Document 001: 10 月の金融政策決定会合は利上げ見送りへ 政策金利の２％超えは？

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 14 日(水)

1/3 
10 月の金融政策決定会合は利上げ見送りへ  
政策金利の２％超えは？ 
 経済調査部主席エコノミスト藤代宏一 
（℡：050-5474-6123）

・10月30日の金融政策決定会合に先立って、早くも観測記事が金融市場を賑わせている。米系
通信は「日銀は今月会合で利上げ見送る公算大、12月の可能性排除せず」と報じた。記事内
では「経済・物価は日銀の見通しに沿って推移しており、２会合連続での利上げが必要なほ
ど物価の上振れリスクが一段と強まっている情勢にはない」、とはいえ「一時的な要因を除
いた基調的な物価上昇率は上昇を続けており、目標の２％から上振れていくリスクには依然
として注意が必要だ」などとする日銀関係者の発言が記述されていた。また既報のとおり、
基調的物価上昇率が概ね２％に達したとの判断を展望レポートに記すことにも触れられてい
た。

・10月の利上げ見送りの根拠として、昨日発表された９月の企業物価指数は一定の存在感があ
る。国内企業物価指数は前月比▲0.3％、前年比＋6.8％と上昇が一服し、市場予想の同＋
7.6％も下回った。原油価格が高止まりするなか、世界的なＡＩ・半導体関連需要の拡大など
を背景に非鉄金属や化学製品などで上昇が続いた一方、米を中心とする農林水産物の価格下
落が下押しに寄与した。円ベースの輸入物価指数は前年比＋24.5％と大幅な上昇を記録した
・日経平均株価は先行き12 ヶ月72,000 円程度で推移するだろう 
・USD/JPY は先行き12 ヶ月 155 円程度で推移するだろう 
・日銀は政策金利を12 月に 1.5％とした後、27 年末までに2.25％とするだろう 
・FED はFF 金利を、年内は4.0％で据え置くだろう

### Page 2

2/3 
が、８月の同＋25.1％からは小幅に鈍化した。川上段階のインフレ圧力は依然として強いも
のの、９月会合時点の判断に大きな修正を迫るものではなかったと考えられる。

・同日発表された「生活意識に関するアンケート調査」（９月調査）も似たような結果であっ
た。５年後の物価予想については「かなり上がる」の割合が51.1％と前回調査対比で概ね横
ばいとなり、「少し上がる」との合計では86.5％と高止まりした。他方、今後５年間の物価
上昇率（年平均）は、中央値が５％で横ばいも、平均値は＋10.1％と前回調査対比で0.7％pt
低下した。ガソリン代の補助に加え、米価の下落が効いたとみられ、家計の予想物価上昇率
が一段と上放れする事態は回避された。政府による家計支援策が効いているとはいえ、家計
の予想物価上昇率が低下したことは、日銀に時間的余裕を与え得る。なお、景況感について
は「１年前との比較」と「１年後の予想」の双方とも大きな変化は認められなかった。

### Page 3

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
3/3 
・先行きの金融政策はどうなるであろうか。筆者は、10月会合は利上げ見送り、12月会合で利
上げを決定し、政策金利は1.5％で越年すると予想する。Ｆｅｄの利上げ観測が燻り、円安圧
力が強いなか、日銀が金融引き締め方向への手を緩めるとなれば、一段と円安が進行しかね
ない。また日銀短観（９月調査）を見る限り、企業の金融環境に大きな変化はみられず、既
往の利上げが実体経済に染み渡っている様子はそれほど窺えない。これらを踏まえ、筆者は
2027年も３～４回の利上げがあり、政策金利は２％を超えることを想定している。

・ただし、Ｆｅｄが2027年に政策金利を据え置き、その次の政策変更が利下げになるとの見方
が生じれば、日銀は円安対策から解放され、利上げを中断ないしは終了する可能性がある。
その点、ウィリアムズ・ＮＹ連銀総裁とウォラー理事の発言は注視しておきたい。まずウィ
リアムズ総裁は10月13日に「来年はインフレがかなり鈍化し、通年でみれば、全体のインフ
レ率は目標⁠の２％近辺になるとみている。これは私が言及した要因、すなわち関税の影響が
完全に過去のものとなることを反映している」として、2027年に利上げは必要ないとの見方
を示した。

・⁠ウォラー理事は10月13日に「最近の米国債利回りの上昇は経済成長の加速とＡＩへの大規模
投資によるもので、インフレ期待の高まりが原因ではない」、「インフレ期待は引き続き
『しっかりと安定している』」と述べ、その上で「データセンター建設を含むＡＩ投資が経
済の需要を支えており、今後２～３年はその効果が続く⁠可能性がある。大幅な生産性向上が
実現するのは、なお『数年先』だ」とした。同理事の発言を整理すると、①予想インフレは
安定しており、その点において現在の金融政策は適切である、②ＡＩ投資が一巡すれば、そ
れに起因するインフレ圧力は鈍化する、③その後に生産性向上によるディスインフレ方向へ
の動きが予想される、といったところであろう。

・両氏の予想が現実のものになれば、Ｆｅｄの利上げ観測は後退し、場合によっては2027年に
利下げ観測が台頭することもあり得る。そうした環境下、日銀は利上げペースを緩める、或
いは終了を検討するなど、多くの選択肢を手にすることができる。

日本：7月以降の設備投資需要はAI関連産業を中心に旺盛
3
 7～9月期の機械受注は増勢回復へ。AI関連業種からの受注がけん引
― ①旺盛なAI需要のもと、関連業種では設備不足感が強まっていたこと、②輸出増が持続するもとで、好業績が期待され
ること、③中東情勢の不確実性が後退したことなどが背景
 先行き設備投資は緩やかな増勢を辿る見込み
― 半導体製造装置をはじめ、AI・データセンター関連品目を中心に受注残が増加しており、目先こうした傾向が続く見通し
― 積み上がった受注残を段階的に消化することで、設備投資が持続的に下支えされる公算が大きい

世界：AI関連投資の拡大が鉱物需要を押し上げる一方、鈍い資源開発投資の回復ペース
4
 AI関連需要の拡大が銅やレアアースの需給環境に影響
― データセンター建設や電力インフラ整備に伴い、銅やレアアースの需要が拡大。ガリウム等の戦略鉱物への需要も増加
 資源開発投資の回復が遅れるなか、中長期的な供給不足への懸念強まる
―IEAは、2035年に銅が供給量対比で約26％の供給不足に陥ると予測。レアアースは供給不足を回避する見込みながら、

採掘・精錬において中国が支配的地位を占めており、供給元の集中がリスク
IEA：銅、レアアースの需要見通し IEA：銅・レアアースの需給バランス予測（2035年）

（％）
世界：AI投資が金融市場を下支えする一方、持続性への懸念は残存
 IMFはAI投資や企業収益の拡大を背景に金融市場が底堅さを維持していると評価
― ただし、金融安定リスクは引き続き高いとの評価で、AI、国債市場、サイバーを主要なリスク要因として指摘
 AIについてはAI主導の市場調整リスクを警戒
―AI中核企業の収益拡大によって株価の割高感は緩和する一方、社債発行や循環的な資金調達が拡大。投資収益が期
待を下回った場合、金融市場への影響が増幅される可能性
5

 主要国の長期金利は、期待短期金利とタームプレミアムの双方から上昇

世界：ヘッジファンドのレバレッジ拡大が市場ショックを増幅、銀行部門への波及にも警戒
 高まるヘッジファンドの存在感と市場への影響力
― ヘッジファンドの総資産は2013年の約4兆ドルから2026年初には約13兆ドルに拡大。平時には市場の流動性や効率性
を支える一方、ストレス時にはレバレッジ解消や取引集中が価格変動を増幅させる懸念

―IMFはヘッジファンドの運用成績悪化がプライムブローカーの収益にも悪影響を及ぼすと分析。銀行自身の健全性に加
え、ノンバンクとの相互連関を通じた金融システム全体への波及にも警鐘

欧州：EU・中国はハイブリッド車の輸入制限で暫定合意。同様の措置は他の品目に広がる可能性
11
 EUと中国は、貿易不均衡の是正措置について暫定合意。中国からのハイブリッド車輸入は従来見通しの半分以下に
―EUが中国産EVに追加関税を賦課した2024年以降、追加関税の対象とならないハイブリッド車輸入が急増
― 欧州委員会の試算では、今回の措置によって2030年までの4年間で数百万台の輸入を削減見込み。直近では、EUが輸
入するハイブリッド車の約半分を中国車が占めており、域内自動車市場に一定のインパクト
 欧州委員会は、幅広い産業で貿易不均衡を是正する必要性を示唆。同様の措置が他の品目にも広がる可能性
― 欧州委員会のモニタリング対象（輸入増加と価格下落が併存する品目）は、中国からの輸入品が最多

― ガソリンは同＋16.6％（前月：同＋9.3％）まで上昇し、ヘッドラインを＋0.47％Pt押し上げ
― 食品・エネルギーを除くコアは同＋1.0％で横ばい。通信機器が同＋14.3％と上昇も、家具・家電は同▲1.8％と下落

 ASEAN5では石油の国家備蓄制度や設備が未整備。石油の安定供給に向けた対応の強化が急務
―ASEANの場合、石油備蓄量は1～2カ月分と限定的。緊急時を見据えた石油備蓄の整備は途上
―10月のAZEC閣僚会合においては、ASEANの石油備蓄拡大や、緊急時の共同備蓄放出に向けた計画などで合意
 電力については域内での需要拡大が見込まれる中、発電能力の増強や送配電網の整備による電力供給の拡大が課題
―IEAは東南アジアの電力需要が2030年まで年平均5.4％増加すると予測。産業や冷房、データセンター需要が押し上げ
― 再エネ含む多様な電源による供給拡大や、AEBFでも議論された国際送配電網の整備や電力融通の取り組みが主眼に

 ASEANの消費は中東情勢緊迫化後に減速も、概して底堅く推移

 背景に各国個別要因や消費マインド悪化の歯止め

― 消費マインドは中東情勢緊迫化後の悪化に歯止め。もっとも、中東危機の長期化が重しに

日本：コメ価格下落で企業物価は鈍化。もっとも、先行きは原油高を背景に再加速へ

 9月の国内企業物価はコメ価格の下落を主因に、前年比＋6.8％（前月：同＋7.6％）と鈍化
― 新米価格が昨年を下回ったこと等を背景に、コメ価格が大幅に下落。国内企業物価を▲0.75%Pt押し下げ
― 一方、3月来の原油高を背景に化学・プラスチック製品は高止まり。また、AI需要等を背景に非鉄金属も高い伸びを維持
 7月以降の原油高再燃を背景に、先行きの国内企業物価は再加速する見込み
― 原油価格高止まりにタンカー運賃の急騰が重なり、先行きの輸入物価は原油関連を中心に加速へ
― 国内企業物価は石油・石炭製品やラグを伴いつつ化学・プラスチック製品などを中心に再加速する見込み

### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 美國 9 月 ISM 非製造業 PMI 從 8 月的 55.4 降至 54.9，顯示服務業活動增速放
緩，然而國內需求強勁使供應鏈承壓，並推動投入價格指數由 72.6 升至 74.0 為
四年多來新高；另一方面，美國 10 月密西根大學消費者信心指數由 9 月的 48.1
降至 46.3，為連續第三個月下降且創 5 月以來最低 ， 惟未來一年通膨預期由 4.6%
升至 4.7%，顯示通膨可能持續維持高位至 2027 年。
⚫ 聯準會 9 月 FOMC 會議紀要顯示與會官員均支持升息，並認為年底前尚需升息
一次 ，但對於後續升息時點及升息理由上存在分歧； 其中部分官員認為升息是防
止能源及其他價格衝擊造成更廣泛影響 的預防性措施，但更為鷹派官員則認為
升息在於抑制投資和支出，以防範正在顯現的需求驅動型通膨。
⚫ 歐洲央行 9 月貨幣政策會議紀要顯示全體決策委員均同意升息，並認為當前經
濟成長風險偏向下行 ， 通膨風險則偏向上行 ，因此將保留政策彈性， 根據經濟數

據做為決策，不提供未來利率路徑指引。
⚫ 台灣 9 月 CPI 年增率自 8 月 2.05%上升至 2.73%，已連續 5 個月超過 2%通膨警
戒線 ， 且創兩年半來最大漲幅 ，除了豪雨導致蔬果價格上漲及中秋假期的短期因
素外，油價居高不下亦使通膨壓力增加。
⚫ 中國央行發布匯率政策立場聲明， 強調中國貿易發展根源於產業國際競爭力的
提升，無必要通過匯率貶值獲取貿易競爭優勢 ，並指出中國長期實施以市場供需
為基礎、參考一籃子貨幣進行調節管理浮動匯率制度。

 美元兌新台幣匯率走勢  
Oct/12/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.699，低點在 31.940，以 31.875 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 4.5 分。 
上週台幣震盪走貶，週初，受到前週公布的美國非農就業數據低於預期，降低
市場對聯準會 10 月升息的預期， 台股創高吸引外資匯入 ， 推升台幣最高來到31.699；
不過隨著美伊緊張情勢升溫且台灣連假將至 ， 台股收低使外資轉為大幅賣超並匯出 ，
推低台幣回到 9 月中以來的震盪區間下緣，最低觸及 31.940，最終在央行調節之下
收在 31.875。 
＜未來分析＞ 
    預估本週台幣區間震盪。就國際市場而言，美國 10 月密西根大學消費者信心
指數持續下滑，由 9 月的 48.1 降至 46.3，創 5 月以來最低，惟未來一年通膨預期
升至 4.7%，加上中東局勢反覆，油價高位震盪，仍支撐市場對於聯準會年內升息
的預期，預計美元指數維持在 102 附近震盪，對台幣帶來壓力；就台灣市場來看，
台灣 9 月出口創歷史新高，年增 60.9%，反映 AI 及科技需求持續強勁，出口商後
續拋匯需求可望提供台幣支撐；然而，台股逼近五萬點關卡之際，外資未能延續買
超走勢，且連假前已大幅賣超台股，若後續外資持續匯出，將限制台幣升值空間。
綜合以上，預估本週台幣區間震盪。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.700-32.000

美元兌人民幣匯率走勢

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週人民幣區間震盪 。 中國十一長假期間 (10/1~10/7) 境內人民幣市場休市 ， 而
境外人民幣維持偏強格局 ， 主要在6.7000 上方震盪 ， 臨近周末 ， 人行中間價為6.7330，
創 3 年多來新高，加上美元指數漲勢放緩，推升境外人民幣突破 6.7000 整數關卡，
終場境外人民幣收在 6.6927，週線升值 134 點，境內人民幣收在 6.6929，較十一長
假前夕升值 121 點。

＜未來分析＞ 
    預估本週人民幣區間偏升。就國際市場來看，美國密西根大學消費者信心指數
由 9 月的 48.1 降至 46.3，創5 月以來新低 ， 不過對於未來一年的通膨預期升至4.7%，
加上油價維持高檔 ， 市場對於聯準會年內升息的預期仍存 ， 美元指數維持高檔震盪 ，
將限制人民幣升值速度；就中國國內市場來看，人行中間價維持偏強設定，不過人
行表示將防範短期大幅波動與急貶風險 ， 並無意透過人民幣貶值取得貿易競爭優勢 ，
有助於穩定市場對人民幣的預期 ；最後就技術面來看 ，USD/CNH 日線圖中 ， 各天期
均線仍向下發散，空頭格局不變，下方支撐先看向 9 月底低點 6.6908。綜合以上，
預估本週人民幣區間偏升。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6800-6.7100

美元兌日圓匯率走勢

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週 USD/JPY 震盪走升。週初，因美元指數在102 附近盤整，帶領USD/JPY 延
續前一週走勢震盪走升到 158 上方；週間，因缺乏重要經濟數據，市場偏向觀望，
USD/JPY 維持在 158 附近震盪；臨近週末，USD/JPY 曾一度回測 157.50 附近，不
過在美日利差的主導下，USD/JPY 依舊保持偏多的走勢，終場收在 158.31，週線上
漲 84 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、路透短觀調查顯示，受半導
體強勁訂單推動，日本 10 月大型製造業信心升至正 22，創近五年新高；但原料成
本高漲重擊非製造業，信心指數降至正 23。與此同時，此前投下升息反對票的日銀
鴿派委員佐藤綾野立場軟化，表態支持分階段升息，市場對後續正常化預期升溫，
預計將限縮 USD/JPY 的上行空間 。二 、 日本首相高市早苗表示密切關注匯率與物價
走勢 ， 強調日本已脫離通縮 ， 將完全尊重央行政策獨立性 ， 暗示不反對進一步升息 。
面對通膨加劇與日圓走貶壓力，其過往堅持的貨幣寬鬆立場已出現轉向。然而目前
美日實質利差仍大，持續為 USD/JPY 提供支撐。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，
USD/JPY 持續沿著上彎的月線上攻，然匯價持續承壓於季線下方，在有效站上季線
及突破前高壓力前，仍以區間震盪看待。綜合以上，預估USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震
盪的可能性較高。

美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
             Oct/12/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週 EUR/USD 震盪 走跌 。週初， 因法國債券風險與區域政治不確定性 ，
EUR/USD 下行最低來到 1.1160； 週間 ，受中東地緣風險升溫與歐洲財政疑慮帶動避
險需求 ， 加上美債殖利率維持高檔 ， 美元利差優勢延續，歐元維持低檔； 臨近週末 ，
逢低歐元有買盤支撐，帶動歐元收復部分跌幅，終場收在 1.1201，週線下跌 46 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間偏空震盪，主要原因如下：一、歐洲央行首席經濟學
家連恩表示，夏末能源成本飆升為經濟增長帶來下行風險，並可能限制央行收緊貨
幣政策的力道，與此同時，歐元區 10 月 Sentix 投資者信心指數由 5.1 降至 2.7，德
國預期指數同步滑落，反映市場對經濟復甦的信心明顯降溫，預計將令歐元持續承
壓。二、法國社會動盪與支出訴求阻礙財政減赤進程，加劇債務可持續性疑慮，並
推升法德公債利差與信用風險，令歐元承壓走弱。相較美國經濟展現韌性且聯準會
維持偏鷹立場，歐洲深陷景氣放緩與財政掣肘，歐洲央行緊縮空間受限。在歐美基
本面與政策前景持續分歧下 ，EUR/USD 缺乏實質支撐 ， 後市將維持偏弱整理並下探
關鍵支撐。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 匯價雖多次向下測試 1.1165 關
鍵價位均獲得支撐，惟每次反彈均無法站上五日線，且各天期均線下彎呈現空頭排
列。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現區間偏空震盪的可能性較高。

力，預估 5 年券區間為 1.85%~1.95%，10 年券區間為 2.0%~2.10%。

### Page 1

Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co.

3Q26 earnings preview: steeper 2nm ramp 
lifting sales upside at resilient margins

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 3,260 TWD | Price: 2,550 TWD

Solid HPC and flagship mobile pipeline solidifies 2H26 
TSMC’s 3Q26 sales were above street +18% QoQ in NT$ and reached US$46.7bn (vs. its 
guide of US$44.6-45.8bn) which we believe should be driven by the builds for Apple’s 
mobile SoC and AMD’s server CPU on 2nm along with multiple GPU and ASIC product 
refreshes migrating to 3nm. The mix upgrades could lift 4Q26 sales by +10-15% QoQ, 
putting it on track to the guidance for 40%+ YoY in ’26. While utilization and pricing are 
favorable, GMs could hold steady in 3Q/4Q26 (BofAe 67.0%/67.8%) rather than expand 
(recent street 67-68%/68-69%) factoring dilution from 2nm and overseas fab ramps. 
Tight supply enables greater value capture and investment 
On solid AI demand outlook, TSMC’s capex should be on track to its lifted guidance of 
US$60-64bn in ’26 (BofAe US$63bn). The investment could see another surge in ’27 
(BofAe US$83bn) for further N3, N2, A16 and CoWoS-L expansion and new technologies 
ramp (e.g. CoPoS and CPO). The tool makers’ modest price hike could require TSMC to 
spend 10% more capex though should be reflected in its value when the capacity starts 
production in ’28 on top of a 5-10% leading edge ASP lift in 1Q27 on supply tightness. 
Checkpoints: AI demand, capex & competition/partnership 
1) TSMC’s view on cloud AI investment amid rate hike + challenging FCF, 2) comment on 
the market and its growth and capex commitment, 3) update on the competition and 
collaboration (see Exhibit 29 for our analysis on Terafab/SpaceX) and US investment.  
AI-led earnings outlook firmer; valuation should catch up 
We lift our 26/27/28 EPS by 2-9% to NT$112/NT$161/NT$203 and keep our Buy rating 
on TSMC and raise PO to NT$3,260/US$620 on 20x ‘27E P/E. Its current valuation is 
undemanding at 16x ‘27E P/E (vs. its long term 11-21x) backed by its advanced 
technology leadership.

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
TSMC was founded in 1987 and is listed in Taiwan 
(2330TT) and the US (TSM/NYS).TSMC is the largest and 
global leader in integrated circuit (IC) manufacturing. As a 
build-to-order foundry, it provides a wide range of value-
add activities: IC manufacturing, mask-making, IC design 
services, turnkey solutions, and process development. We 
attribute its success to its proven, winning business model, 
unparalleled scale advantage, optimized execution, and 
technology scope and depth. 5 shares = 1 ADR.

Investment Rationale 
The ongoing semi content growth in mobile, rise of artificial 
intelligence (AI), and proliferation of Internet of Things (IoT) 
should result in sustainable upside in aggregate computing 
power globally. TSMC, as the leading contract manufacturer 
of semiconductor chips, is in a good position to capitalize. 
We are structurally positive on TSMC and expect it to 
sustain over 15% growth with rising FCF from 2026 
onwards.

### Page 4

3Q26 sales topped guidance; GMs tracking
well but recent street could be too bullish
We profile our expectation for TSMC’s 3Q26 earnings on October 15th. 3Q26 sales were
reported +18% QoQ in NT$, above BofAe/street expectations of +14%/+13% QoQ.
Based on the average FX of NT$31.99/US$ (vs. its NT$32US$ guide), its sales in US$
term would be US$46.7bn, also above its guidance range of US$44.6-45.8bn. we believe
the strength is led by strong ramp on 2nm potentially growing to 11% of its sales in

3Q26 (vs. 3% in 2Q26) for Apple’s builds for its product launches and surging demand
for AMD’s Venice server CPU. These growth drivers on the node are supplemented by
the incremental inventory stocking from Mediatek and Qualcomm for their flagship
smartphone SoCs albeit at a low base and below seasonal due to weak Android demand.
On top of the 2nm scale up, we expect the utilization for 5nm and 3nm to stay above
100% backed by the continued HPC/AI build strength especially with multiple product

upgrades including 1) accelerators (GPUs and ASICs): strong demand for Nvidia’s Rubin
GPU, AMD’s MI400, AWS’s Trainium3 ramp and initial ramp for Google’s TPUv8t/i more
than offsetting a mild wind down in Google’s Ironwood TPU, 2) CPUs: solid demand for
general purpose servers and AI data centers as noted in our deep dive report quantifying
CPU demand through ’30 across x86 (AMD’s Turin CPUs) and ARM based CPUs (e.g.
Nvidia’s Vera CPU, Google’s Axion CPU, AWS Graviton CPU along with incremental

contribution from Microsoft Cobalt CPU) and 3) networking switch: more and faster
data transmission in the complex AI data centers requires more advanced  networking
switch solutions from Nvidia/Mellanox, Broadcom and Marvell.
The company’s better than expected sales in 3Q26 backed by healthy shipment and
pricing trend on the advanced nodes should be supportive to its GMs. However, in
contrast to the recent aggressive street expectations of 67-68% GMs in 3Q26, we only

model it at the high-end of its guidance of 65-67%, balancing more favorable utilization
and gradual 3nm depreciation roll off partially offset by a 3-4% GMs dilution from 2nm
ramp which we believe is driver for the incremental sales during the quarter.

### Page 5

HPC/Apple lifting 4Q26 sales outlook;
resilient GM expectation but below street
For 4Q26, based on its slightly above +40% YoY full year sales guide, TSMC’s sales could
grow 10-15% QoQ (BofAe +11%), better than its 10-year seasonality of 7% QoQ even
off a high base from 1Q-3Q26. The blended ASPs strength (BofAe +11% QoQ) should be
the key driver for 4Q26 sales as shipment mix continues to shift toward advanced nodes,
with revenue from 5nm and below processes representing 75% of the company’s sales

(vs. 66%/71% in 2Q/3Q26). Specifically, 2nm with 15%+ higher wafer pricing than 3nm
could further grow its contribution to 18% of TSMC’s sales during the quarter. On the
other hand, we model a stable wafer shipment (BofAe +9%/-5% QoQ for
advanced/mature nodes), balancing HPC demand strength and Apple’s seasonal chipset
builds for its new product launches and a modest drop on the specialty process output
and effective capacity loss due to equipment movement for the node conversion.

While the blended pricing is even more favorable and utilization on the advanced and
mature nodes in general stay healthy, we model a relatively stable GMs only up to
~67.8% levels in 4Q26 (vs. recent street and investors’ expectations at 68-69%). The key
factors we consider as mildly negative offset would be 1) higher depreciation: +25-30%
HoH in 2H26 due to more overseas and 2nm capacity ramp and 2) 2nm still going
through yield learning curve and could see more meaningful impact in 4Q26 (vs. its 3-4%

GMs dilution guide in 2H26) as the sales contribution grows.

### Page 6

2026-27 sales and margins outlook 
solidified by healthy AI demand 
Factoring in stronger CPU and AI GPU/ASIC demand outlook as noted in our Semicon 
Taiwan takeaways report where we updated our industry models, we are lifting our US$ 
sales expectation for TSMC in ‘26/’27 to +42%/+40% YoY respectively (vs. BofA prior 
+41%/+40% YoY). More importantly, with utilization staying tighter for longer along with 
inflationary cost pass through (e.g. equipment and consumables), TSMC should raise its 
advanced node and packaging pricing by 5-10% in the beginning of 2027 to reflect its 
value. This along with a mild 3-5% price hike on the mature nodes where the utilization 
is back to 90-95% as it optimizes the product mix following the strategic 6”/8” capacity 
retirement should lift its GMs to 67%/69% in 2026/27 (vs. BofA prior 67%/67%).

Capex on track for lifted guide for 2026 
and will grow 20%+ CAGR from ’26-28 
We lifted our front-end foundry industry capex growth expectation to 19%/26% YoY for 
‘26/27 to US$86bn/US$108bn, supporting a CAGR of 16% from ’26-28. This is mostly 
led by our higher expectation on TSMC’s capex on stronger wafer demand on N3 and N2 
from GPUs, ASICs, CPUs, networking switches and HBM base dies and initial ramp of 
A16, along with incremental spending reacceleration from tier 2 foundries. 
Separately, we also raised the back-end packaging and testing industry spending 
estimates from 66%/36% YoY to 82%/21% YoY for ‘26/27 to US$27bn/US$33bn, 
supporting a CAGR of 15% from ’26-28. The upward revision is driven by our higher 
expectation of TSMC’s more aggressive capacity addition on CoWoS-R/L to 
accommodate more demand from its customers amid growing level of chipset 
complexity and reticle size and higher level of capacity readiness of SoIC for higher 3D 
packaging adoption for logic and memory dies integration and CPO. The more 
aggressive spending from ASE is set to grow by another 30-50% YoY in 2027 off a high 
base in 2026 on its own FoCoS capacity ramp and TSMC’s CoWoS licensing.

Specifically on TSMC, based on our bottom up capacity analysis across front-end 
manufacturing and back-end packaging, TSMC’s capex spending is tracking to US$63bn 
for 2026 (vs. its latest guidance of US$60-64bn) mostly skewed toward advanced front-
end manufacturing including 55-60k WPM 3nm capacity addition, 60k WPM 2nm 
capacity ramp, initial 3-5k WPM A16 capacity installation. The back-end packaging 
investment should be largely driven by its CoWoS capacity growth while the spending on 
the equipment pull-in for CPO and CoPoS remains limited as most of the equipment are 
still under sampling process for qualification and meaningful shipment will be in 2027-
28. 
In addition to the earlier tool pull-in schedule due to tighter equipment supply with 
memory makers expanding, the fast-growing capex should reflect the rising cost per 1k 
WPM on the advanced nodes vs. mature nodes, with the equipment cost per 1k WPM 
advanced wafer capacity expansion has grown from US$156mn in 2023 to US$241mn in 
2025. Even factoring in more common use of equipment on the advanced nodes, the 
capex/1k WPM advanced capacity could further rise by 20% to US$289mn in 2027 with 
the technology architecture upgrade. 
On top of the investment in the core front-end manufacturing, we expect the company 
to slightly outspend in the back-end packaging and estimate it is at the upper half of its 
guidance for 10-20% total capex, mainly for more aggressive capacity expansion to 
meet its customers’ demand amid growing competition from Intel’s EMIB-T solution and 
technology innovation. On the other hand, the maintenance capex could be reduced for 
specialty technologies as the company gradually retires the equipment.

With TSMC’s stronger bargaining power against the equipment supply chain vs. its

its sales of +32% CAGR, sustaining GMs expansion to 68-69% levels by 2028.

### Page 10

Capacity expansion accelerates across 
advanced front-end and back-end 
As TSMC is in the process to retire a portion of its equipment on 6”, 8” and mature 12” 
processes while still adding the capacity on the advanced nodes, we estimate the 
company’s total front-end manufacturing capacity will drop by 2% YoY in 2026. 
Exhibit 17: TSMC's year-end capacity by node for 20nm and below 
TSMC will expand aggressively on 3nm and 2nm through 2027

Source: Company Data, BofA Global Research estimates 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

Exhibit 18: TSMC's advanced node capacity as % of its installed base 
TSMC's advanced nodes will reach 40% of its total capacity

Source: Company Data, BofA Global Research estimates 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH

The trend could be more stable into 2027-28 as TSMC strives to optimize its capacity 
mix and across node conversion to accommodate its customers’ demand especially on 
the leading-edge technologies, with its advanced capacity set to grow by 10% CAGR 
from 2025-28 balanced by the mature capacity reduction at -8% CAGR. 
Based on our estimate, TSMC’s capacity for 7nm and below nodes will grow from 320k 
WPM per month in 2023 to ~700k WPM by 2028 at a 16% CAGR, through expansion and 
efficient conversion from higher equipment commonality, cross technology planning and 
AI productivity gains (e.g. tool idle time and chamber cleaning schedule optimization). 
This will also support the mix of 7nm and below capacity climb toward 40-45% of its 
total capacity (vs. 24% in 2023). By region, we expect the capacity expansion through 
2027 to enable its overseas fabs to account for 17% of TSMC’s total capacity by 2028 
(vs. 7% in 2020) and similarly for the advanced node capacity (vs. ~0% in 2023), mainly 
in Arizona and Kumamoto. 
Specifically, we believe the company is on track to its target on 25% CAGR capacity 
expansion for its 5nm and 3nm from ’22-27. We model its 3nm capacity will reach 
190k/230k WPM by 4Q26/27. On N2 family, TSMC plans to grow capacity by 70% CAGR 
from ’26-28, with 1st year ramp delivering 45% more capacity vs. 3nm as it rolls out 5 
fabs at once.

We would note 3nm, as a sweet spot for high performance computing, networking and

On the back-end packaging, with surging server CPU demand on top of the already 
strong order backlog for GPUs and ASICs, we estimate the industry’s CoWoS supply will 
need to grow by 52% CAGR from 2026-28. TSMC will still lead the capacity expansion 
from 120k WPM in 4Q26 to 210k WPM in 4Q27, while the OSATs will also grow their 
portion of the supply with ASE and Amkor adding capacity to 70-75k+ WPM (vs. 30-35k 
in 4Q26) and 25-30k WPM (vs. 15-20k by 4Q26) in 4Q27 respectively. Specifically, while 
TSMC should still lead the supply for complex GPU and ASIC packaging, ASE grows as a 
major 2nd source with its capacity share growing from 18% of the industry in ’26 to 29% 
in ’28 and proven track record as the major CoWoS full process service provider for 
AMD’s Venice server CPU.

### Page 14

Solid technology roadmap and execution 
easing competition concern 
TSMC is pursuing a dual track strategy for its front-end manufacturing, advancing the 
solution for both its consumer and HPC customers. The nodes targeted for consumer 
chipsets requiring cost efficiency and IP reuse will be introduced on an annual basis to 
match its customers’ yearly product launch schedule. For the HPC applications, the 
company targets to roll out a new node with biennial cadence, supporting major leaps in 
performance, transistor density, and power density. 
Exhibit 27: Front-end manufacturing roadmap comparison 
TSMC's advanced node technology roadmap advancing faster vs. Intel and Samsung and has the widest offerings targeted for different applications

Source: BofA Global Research estimates 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH 
This is evidenced in its N2 family and below nodes, with N2U ramping in ’28 for mobile 
applications and A13 node in ’29 which will be backward compatible to A14. It will also 
have A12 node in ’29 integrated SPR to address power integrity and current delivery 
constraint suitable for AI/HPC systems. The strategy along with the continued expansion 
of the derivative offerings for main nodes should position TSMC well to defend its 
market share with its competitors claiming their progress on the advanced 
manufacturing. 
Specifically, Intel’s 18A has been in mass production since 4Q25 for its Panther Lake 
and Clearwater Forest chipsets though yield will not be fully fine tuned until 1Q27 at 25-
30k WPM capacity scale in its Oregon and Arizona fabs. The follow on derivative 
technology, 18A-P, has recently started risk production featuring 9% performance gains 
though may not enter into mass production until 2027 at the earliest. 
Longer term, Intel’s next major upgrade will be on 14A which is guided to start risk 
production in 2028 for commercialization in 2029. It is important to note A14 will be the 
first major node Intel provides with its external foundry customers and 0.9 PDK is 
targeted to be shipped in 4Q26. 
For Samsung, at its recent SAFE Forum in 2Q26, the company reaffirmed four 
generations of 2nm roadmap, including SF2 which has been in production since 2025, 
SF2P in 2026, SF2P+ in 2027-28 and SF2X (no firm date). Notably, the previously 
announced derivative technologies, including SF2Z featuring backside power for HPC 
applications and SF2A for automotive applications, were not included in its latest 
roadmap which should be folded as part of its SF2P+ offerings. With a modest delay for 
its 2nm progress, the company also pushed out its SF1.4 mass production to 2029 (vs. 
original 2027). 
On the capacity and yield planning for its advanced nodes, Samsung noted its SF2 yield 
is still at 50-60% levels but confirmed its SF2P+ tooling has been in installation through 
2026 for mass production in 2027 in its Taylor fab. Its capacity expansion in the US 
along with the existing 20k WPM capacity on SF2 in Korea put the company’s capacity 
installed base on the node at 35k WPM by the end of 2027.

### Page 15

Exhibit 28: TSMC's front-end manufacturing transistor density vs. Intel 
TSMC is widening its transistor density advantage against Intel as technology advances

Source: BofA Global Research estimates 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH 
Based on the capacity planning and technology roadmap, we believe TSMC retains the 
leadership through its roadmap and yield execution and scale. Specifically, the 
company’s transistor density has been outperforming by ~30% for its N3/N3E vs. Intel 
3/18A. With similar production timing and targeted applications, we estimate the 
transistor density gap could be even widened to ~50%/~60% for TSMC’s upcoming 
A16/A12 nodes when comparing with Intel 18A-P/14A. 
In addition to delivering superior specs on the power, performance and area, TSMC’s 
execution reliability has become self-reinforcing: superior yield and on-time delivery on 
the leading edge technologies win customer trust. The profitable business opportunity 
then translates the cash flow funds to the next leg of capex cycle required for further 
technology and capacity lead. In contrast to TSMC’s 150-200k+ WPM capacity scale on 
every major node at mature yield, Samsung and Intel running their business at 15-20% 
of the company’s scale at 60-70% yield is not sufficient to meet their own demand nor 
the stringent requirement from the external customers especially as the opportunity cost 
gets higher on the advanced manufacturing. 
On the back-end packaging and testing solutions, TSMC provides its customers with 
higher complexity of the system level integration across advanced logic dies, CPO 
components and HBM through its CoWoS and SoIC technologies for the compute 
density and efficiency optimization. To accommodate larger size package, it is rolling out 
5.5-reticle CoWoS in ’26 with yield reaching 98%+ and will push the technology to 
9.5x/14x reticle limit in ’27/’28 
Longer term, the company is developing SoW for up to 40-reticle packaging and CoPoS 
potentially for late ‘28 / early ’29 mass deployment. The company is also upgrading CPO 
with COUPE solutions from PCB and substrate to interposer for 10x power efficiency 
uplift and 20x latency reduction though the contribution would still be limited based on 
our estimate at least through ’27 before accelerating to 1% of its sales in ’28 with more 
scale up switch adoption. 
While CoPoS mass ramp is toward ’29, the CoWoS upgrade at good yield should keep its 
position vs. Intel’s EMIB-T currently at 80-85% pilot yield seeing risk of losing business 
if it fails to deliver 95%+ mass production yield by mid-’27.

### Page 16

SpaceX could be an anchor customer for
TSMC, but not co-owning capacity/tech
On top of the competition risk from the incumbent players, the headline of Elon Musk’s
aggressiveness to build Terafab targeting long term 1 terawatt equivalent chipset
capacity for AI compute deployment in the space term also triggers the investor
concerns on TSMC’s positioning. We believe the risk is overstated as there could be a
delay in the qualification process of Intel’s 18A for external customers and 14A due to IP

and manufacturing flow issues based on the recent supply chain feedback. In addition,
the equipment vendors also noted Terafab is only evaluating on equipment spec and
potential procurement rather than placing firm orders. With extending equipment
delivery lead time, this could put the company in an unfavorable position to receive tools
for the initial production ramp in ’29 it scheduled.
In the mid-term, Terafab’s applications under Texas’s JETI program earlier this year

outlined ~US$119bn of planned investment across 4 phases from ’26-36, with ~60% of
it on tool procurement. The spending in ’26-28 would be highly skewed toward facility
construction before it starts to pull in US$6-8bn equipment per annum from ‘29-36.
We estimate the company will need to spend US$600mn+ for every 1k WPM expansion
on the advanced nodes it targets in the US. Factoring the investment it targets to spend
in the next decade, Terafab could only grow its capacity installed base by ~10k WPM per

annum at maximum to 96k WPM front-end manufacturing capacity installed by ’36, well
below TSMC’s scale, even if it can successfully deliver the technology roadmap through
its own R&D effort and Intel partnership. This would prevent Terafab’s operation from
the economies of scale as the node migration continues, keeping TSMC as the major
foundry partner for Tesla/SpaceX.
Exhibit 29: Wafer supply/demand analysis on Elon Musk's AI deployment target
With Terafab's near term delay and limited long term scale, TSMC will continue to be critical for Musk's ambitious AI compute deployment plan

BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH
We also analyze the wafer consumption from SpaceX’s annual orbital AI deployment
target in ‘28/’29 of 10GW/100GW and Musk’s long term 1TW target. Assuming the

compute it plans to deploy in ’28-29 is all powered by the most advanced Nvidia’s
Feynman GPUs delivering 6kW per package, this would drive 1.7mn/16.7mn chipset
demand in ’28/’29 respectively which requires 10k/101k monthly advanced wafer
production. From the supply perspective, as Feynman GPU will be built on TSMC’s A16
node, the chipset demand should be fully accommodated. With limited support from
Terafab, SpaceX in this scenario could become one of the largest customers for TSMC

consuming ~20% of its advanced node capacity in ‘29 (vs. ~1% in ’26 and ~2% in ’28).
Longer term, Musk’s ambitious target to deploy 1 terawatt AI compute power annually
would consume 1mn+ wafers per month on the advanced nodes. To have higher level of
the control on capacity sufficiency, Terafab could consider to partner with TSMC though
the likelihood of TSMC licensing technology or operating a fab with Terafab holding a
meaningful share should be low. Specifically, we would note TSMC’s JV fab record is

limited only with WaferTech, SSMC, VIS, JASM, ESMC and AVSM mostly on mature
nodes and it did not co-own a fab with Apple who contributed ~21% of its revenue from
’15-25 mostly on the advanced processes as it keeps full business control and preserves
customer neutrality.

TSMC’s current valuation remains well supported when viewed through both its

### Page 18

Specifically, the valuation deviation for TSMC’s HPC customers vs. the company has 
widened with 100-150% valuation premium vs. TSMC at this stage (vs. historical 
average of 53%). We believe this is driven by a surge in valuation for the direct 
beneficiaries from AI data center deployment, including the companies designing GPUs, 
ASICs and networking chipsets. 
Exhibit 34: TSMC's consensus earnings vs. P/E trend 
TSMC's share price strength only driven by earnings upgrade so far

Source: BofA Global Research, Bloomberg 
BofA GLOBAL RESEARCH 
It is also noteworthy that, while TSMC’s share performance since 2H23 has been solid, it 
is predominantly driven by the earnings upward revisions rather than multiple expansion. 
The company’s NTM P/E has remained largely stable, only lifted to 18x in the past 2 
years vs. its long-term ~17x levels. The company’s ADR though has been performing 
better as it attracts a broader investor base vs. common share, with its premium still at 
10% (vs. the long term average 8%) and at 20x 2027 consensus P/E. 
With its firmer technology leadership, this should bode well for the company’s earnings 
upgrade cycle and its share should also be re-rated at least 20x NTM P/E (24x P/E for 
ADR factoring the premium), back closer to its customers and the broader ecosystem.

### Page 22

Price objective basis & risk 
Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMWF / TSM) 
Our PO for TSMC is NT$3,260 per share (US$620 per ADR), based on 20x 2027E P/E. 
TSMC's valuation has traded in the range of 10-26x PE from 2017. 20x is in the mid to 
high end of the historical range, above the average P/E of 16x. We think a PE above the 
average is justified given TSMC's enhanced industry position and mid-to-high 50% 5-
year CAGR in AI should underpin a rerating

Downside risks to our price objective are: (1) greater-than-expected slowdown in global 
smartphone/consumer electronics demand, (2) Intel's potential insourcing strategy and 
ambitions in foundry service, (3) execution risks on advanced nodes, and (4) higher than 
expected tariffs.

3Q26Pre: 환율 하락에도 성장 흐름 유지 
매출액 1조 3,477억원(+7.2%YoY, 이하 YoY 생략), 영업이익 5,957억원(-6.0%, OPM 
44.2%)로 컨센서스에 부합할 것으로 전망. 3분기 원달러 평균 환율은 1,423원으로 전분기 
평균 환율 대비 약 5.2% 하락에도 불구하고 성장 흐름 지속. 
2분기 노조 파업으로 인한 영향은 3분기 생산을 진행했으며 4분기 매출에 반영될 예정으로 
연간 실적에 미치는 영향은 제한적일 것으로 예상. GSK로부터 인수한 록빌 공장도 안정적
으로 생산이 이루어지고 있으며 CAPA 여유분은 추가 수주를 진행 중으로 28~29년 이후 
완전 가동 전망. 
4Q, 6공장 건설과 폴리펩타이드 인수 
유상 증자 관련 투자 설명서 상 투자 계획에 따르면 6공장 투자는 26년부터 29년까지 진행 
될 예정으로 29년 가동 목표. 6공장은 5공장과 동일한 18만 리터 규모로 건설되며, 연내 투
자 집행을 시작함에 따라 건설 발표도 임박한 것으로 추정. 동사는 일정 수준 이상 기존 공
장 수주를 확보한 후 후속 공장 건설을 진행해오고 있어 non-binding 수주 계약의 본계약 
전환도 순차적으로 이루어질 것으로 전망. 
폴리펩타이드 인수도 현재 공개 매수를 진행 중에 있으며 연내 마무리될 것으로 예상. 
1H26 폴리펩타이드 매출은 2.37억 유로(+41.6%)로 GLP-1 agonist 등 펩타이드 의약품 
수요 증가에 따라 높은 성장 지속. 
투자 의견 Buy, 목표 주가 200만원으로 하향 
매수 의견을 유지하며 목표 주가는 기존 210만원에서 200만원으로 하향. 목표 주가 하향은 
유상 증자에 따른 EPS 희석에 기인. 글로벌 항체 CDMO 수요가 여전히 견조하며, 폴리펩
타이드 인수로 펩타이드로의 확장에 따른 추가적인 성장 기대. 주주 대상 유상 증자 영향과 
파업 우려 등이 있으나 노사 합의 시 주가 반등 전망.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
As was universally expected, the RBA Monetary Policy
Board (MPB) lifted the cash rate by 25bps to 4.6% at its
September 2026 meeting.
This is quite the turnaround from the 11 August meeting,
where an extended period on hold looked to be the base case.

At that time, the data had broken against the RBA’s more
hawkish view, and its published forecasts did not support
further hikes. But the MPB can now point to the emergence of
some of the upside risks to inflation it warned of in August.
The post-meeting statement was notable for its emphasis
on the Middle East conflict, noting that “global energy prices
are now much higher than had been assumed in the August
forecasts” – “much higher” is stronger language than one

normally sees in an RBA statement. The RBA’s liaison indicates
that firms are passing this on or planning to do so. The pass-
through was characterised as partial, and additional to
domestically driven inflation arising from capacity pressures. We
read this as implying that the RBA is concerned about upside
risks to underlying inflation in Q4 of this year, as well as Q3.
The RBA is also wary about pressure on both construction
costs and retail prices of IT goods arising from the AI/data

centre boom, although in the media conference, the Governor
clarified that risks there were building rather than materialising.
The broader background highlighted in the Governor’s opening
statement to the media conference, is of demand outstripping
supply. The statement evidenced this by noting the stronger
July CPI and June quarter GDP results than expected.
The accompanying statement was notable for the absence of
language about the labour market being a little tight, despite

this being the inter-meeting message from the Governor
and other staff (see, for example, the Governor’s opening
statement on 18 September) and the language in the post-
meeting media conference. Views clearly differ on this: we
note that the Treasury forecasts underlying the May budget
and the recent Intergenerational Review imply both more
spare capacity in the labour market (a lower NAIRU) and faster
growth in potential from wherever the starting point is (higher

productivity growth and trend increase in participation).
Instead, the post-meeting statement characterised the
labour market as easing “broadly as expected”, despite the
unemployment rate already rising higher than the August SMP
forecast of 4.5% for the December quarter.
RBA hikes in September, November follow-up now expected
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
The decision was unanimous, suggesting that any qualms
external MPB members might have had about the RBA’s

downbeat view of supply capacity were overruled by resurgent
oil prices and the stronger than forecast CPI and GDP data.
With upside risks being seen to have emerged, the post-meeting
statement language included that the Board “will continue to
do what it considers necessary to bring inflation sustainably
back to target, including increasing the cash rate target further if
needed”, a small but meaningful change from the “if upside risks
materialise” language from the previous meeting, and back to

the tone of the language at the June meeting.
The bar for a follow-up hike in November is low. Indeed,
judging by the post-meeting rhetoric, a November hike is now
the base case, absent a lasting resolution of the Middle East
conflict beforehand, or some other event that significantly
lowers the outlook for energy-related costs in Australia. The
bar for hikes beyond that is much higher, given the cumulative
rise in interest rates, and noting that the labour market is

easing and the housing market will likely weaken further.
The August inflation data released the day after the meeting
was in line with our expectations. We therefore continue to
expect Q3 trimmed mean inflation around 0.9%qtr, too high for
the RBA’s comfort, and upside risks for Q4. Likewise, there are
no implications for our rates view from this week’s inflation data.
Forecast changes
• We have updated our financial forecasts to reflect the change

in our rate view.
• Our labour market forecasts are unchanged on the first
decimal place, but there is some modest upside risk to our
unemployment forecast.
• Our updated GDP profile can be found on page 9.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: searching for signal
led the correction to date, the fact that the other capitals
are also reporting price declines clearly indicates that the
correction is deepening and broadening, as higher interest
rates collide with the tax policy changes announced in May’s

budget. Sentiment effects indeed remain at play, as evinced
by the continued pull-back in investor credit growth . Westpac
anticipates a peak-to-trough decline of around 7%, with tight
on-market supply limiting the extent of the correction. For
more, see our latest Housing Pulse.
Offshore, there was plenty of data to dissect.
In the US, inflation eased in August despite ongoing strength
in household demand and higher energy prices. Headline PCE
inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.4%yr, while core PCE increased

0.2%mth and 3.0%yr. Encouragingly for the FOMC, the
share of PCE components rising by more than 3% over the
past year, a measure frequently cited by Fed Chairman Kevin
Warsh, declined to 45% from 49%, suggesting some easing in
broad-based inflation pressures. At the same time, household
spending remained robust, increasing 0.9%mth, equivalent
to around 0.6%mth in real terms. That said, personal income
growth was soft, rising 0.2% in the month, signalling

households opted to draw down savings.
The ISM manufacturing survey was stronger than the
headline suggested. While the index edged down 0.1pt to
54.5, manufacturing has now expanded for nine consecutive
months. There were some signs of capacity constraints
emerging, with production softened in the month but backlogs
up 4.6pts to 56.4, suggesting that firms struggled to keep
pace with demand. New orders rose 1.6pts to 55.3, primarily
due to domestic demand as new export orders eased, backlogs

increased. Firms responded by lifting employment, 1.5pts to
52.7. Against this backdrop manufacturers saw a renewed
acceleration in cost pressures, with the prices paid index
jumping 6.8pts to 77.9. Firms cited higher steel and aluminium
prices, elevated energy costs, rising freight charges and
ongoing semiconductor shortages, with every major industry
reporting higher input costs during the month.
The past week’s data will have done nothing to bridge the

clear divide across the FOMC, as evident in this week’s busy
Fedspeak. Some participants argued for further rate hikes,
citing strong inflation, diminished supply capacity and policy
that is not yet restrictive. Others counselled patience, pointing
to progress on inflation and that September’s hike buys time
to assess upcoming data. The one thing both agree on is that
inflation will be the primary determinant of the timing and
extent of further rate hikes. Our view remains that one further

hike is likely this cycle to bring policy closer to neutral.
In Australia, this week’s main event was the RBA Monetary
Policy Board’s (MPB) decision to raise the cash rate by 25bps
to 4.60%. This outcome was universally expected given
Governor Bullock had already communicated that upside risks
to inflation had materialised – the conditionality established
by the MPB back in August for considering another rate hike.
Governor Bullock made explicit note of three upside risks.

The Middle East conflict was emphasised as the risk that had
“materialised”, given the re-escalation and renewed surge in crude
oil and refined product prices over recent months. The RBA’s
liaison warns this could lead to further pass-through of costs
and more persistent inflation pressures. On top of this, domestic
capacity pressures and the data centre/AI investment boom were
also noted as upside risks that the MPB remain alert to.
In a video update mid-week, Chief Economist Luci Ellis

explains why the bar for a follow-up rate hike in November is
low. We now expect a 25bp increase in November, provided
there is no sudden resolution of the Middle East conflict
beforehand, or some other event that significantly lowers the
outlook for energy-related costs.
Subsequently, the August CPI revealed that underlying
(trimmed mean) inflation rose 0.2% (3.4%yr), which was
slightly below the market’s expectation but spot on Westpac’s
forecast. As usual, the monthly detail was mixed, but the key

drivers of underlying inflation remain centred on housing-
related components, particularly rents and new dwelling
purchases. Headline inflation remained volatile, with the
unwind of the fuel excise cut causing another spike in fuel
prices. We have maintained our forecast for a 0.9% lift in
trimmed mean inflation in Q3, and with upside risks building
for Q4, a follow-up rate hike in November appears most likely.
Regarding the current growth pulse, household spending

surprised with a flat reading in August. Services spend fell back
following recent strength that was driven by major sporting
events, but this was offset by an increase in goods spend,
particularly in fuel and electric vehicles. Together with our
latest card data for early September, the consumer spending
pulse is shading a touch softer. However, another solid
outcome for business investment could once again be on the
cards for Q3. August’s goods trade data showcased another

significant spike in data centre-related equipment imports, up
$4.3bn, similar in scale to Q1. While clearly import-driven, our
modelling suggests that the local economic impact of such
investment is likely to be around half of the total investment.
Before moving offshore, a final note on housing. Cotality’s
latest update revealed that dwelling prices fell another 1.1%
nationally in September. While Sydney and Melbourne have
Sian Fenner, Head of Business and Industry Economics

Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

### Page 4

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
In China, policymakers announced another round of targeted
support measures aimed at stabilising growth and supporting
the struggling property sector. The PBOC lowered rates on its
Pledged Supplementary Lending facility by 25bps to 1.5% and
expanded quotas for targeted lending programs. Authorities

also introduced subsidies for eligible first-home buyers. The
subsidy applies to homes with a floor area of 120m² or less
and a value below CNY1.5mn, with support capped at 1ppt of
mortgage costs, up to CNY10,000 and for a maximum term of
five years. The measures complement reforms announced in
August to improve developer financing conditions and support
housing completion. However, weak credit demand and
confidence remains a significant constraint, suggesting policy

transmission is still limited.
The September PMI data pointed to a modest improvement
in activity, although the details were less convincing.
Manufacturing returned to expansionary territory, rising 0.9pts
to 50.1, supported by stronger production and new orders.
Much of the improvement, however, reflected a rebound in
prices, with the raw materials price index rising 7.6pts and
producer prices increasing 6.2pts. Labour market conditions
remained weak, with the employment index falling a further

0.6pts and remaining below 50 for almost four years. Conditions
in the non-manufacturing sector also improved, with the
services PMI lifting from 49.0 to 50.2, driven largely by stronger
construction activity. Input and output prices moved higher,
selling prices returned back above 50, export orders improved
to 48.5 and employment increased 0.5pts, although at 45.9 it
remains firmly in contractionary territory. Overall, the surveys
suggest activity is stabilising and policy support is gaining some

traction, but weak labour demand, subdued credit growth and
soft domestic demand indicate the recovery remains fragile.
In Japan, the September Tankan survey delivered another
constructive signal on the economy and reinforced the case
for further policy normalisation. Business conditions improved
by 2pts for large manufacturers and 5pts more broadly, while
profitability indicators strengthened and capital expenditure
intentions remained elevated. Large manufacturers expect

fixed investment to increase by 11.6% in FY2026, while large
non-manufacturers expect investment growth of 11.2%,
pointing to continued confidence in the medium-term outlook.
Capacity indicators improved further across both
manufacturing and non-manufacturing sectors, with measures
of excess production capacity becoming less negative
and indicating that demand remains sufficiently strong to
keep pressure on existing capacity. Labour shortages also

intensified, with employment conditions becoming more
negative across most industries and firm sizes, pointing to a
progressively tighter labour market. Inflation expectations
remained anchored around 2%, providing further evidence
that Japan is moving towards a more sustainable inflation
environment. Overall, the survey was consistent with an
economy operating close to capacity and supports the view
that a December rate hike remains a realistic possibility.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Resilient activity and simmering inflation
While bumpy and uneven, New Zealand’s economic
recovery has continued over the past few months, with
activity showing resilience in the face of continued global
headwinds. Under the surface, however, inflation pressures

are bubbling away, with much of that pressure centred on
parts of the economy that aren’t sensitive to interest rates.
Economic activity getting back on firmer footing
Looking first at recent activity data, the economy has been
finding its feet again after taking a knock in the wake of the
Middle East war. That has been seen most clearly in the jobs
market, with the number of filled jobs rising by 0.9% in the
year to August. While not shooting the lights out, that is still a

noticeable improvement from the early part of the year, when
filled job numbers were falling.
Under the surface, it’s still a very mixed picture. Agriculture
remains the main engine of growth, with the strength in
commodity export earnings helping to boost incomes,
confidence and spending in regions with strong rural backbones.
That’s supported firm job growth in regions like Canterbury
(where filled job numbers are up 1.9% over the past year), Otago

(+1.5%) and Southland (+3.1%). In contrast, jobs growth remains
much more moderate in Auckland (up 0.6%) and Wellington
(up just 0.4%). That town-and-country split is likely to remain a
feature of the economic landscape for some time yet.
Looking ahead, businesses expect trading conditions to
remain resilient through the final months of the year. That
was reflected in September’s Business Outlook survey.
While the survey’s gauges of expected business activity and

general business confidence eased over the past month, they
remain at firm levels, consistent with continued moderate
growth through the latter part of the year. That resilience has
also been evident in the household sector, with consumer
confidence largely steady through September.
The resilience in household and business sentiment is notable,
as fuel prices have taken a big step higher over the past month.
The average price of 91 unleaded has risen by around 49c/litre –

albeit late in the month and likely not fully reflected in surveyed
confidence – taking it back to the levels seen during the early
stages of the Middle East conflict. Those higher fuel prices
have taken a bite out of household disposable incomes, with
spending on fuel up about 22% compared with this time last
year, even though the volume of fuel sold is down around 11%.
But even with the lift in fuel prices, we haven’t seen the same
degree of pessimism as earlier in the year, when fuel prices first

spiked. Notably, those higher fuel prices haven’t spilled over
into a broader lift in output price pressures either (at least at
this stage). Inflation expectations were steady in September’s
household and business surveys. Similarly, the number of firms
planning to raise prices fell back despite a lift in wage and cost
expectations. However, while that stability in pricing gauges is
welcome, measures of inflation pressure remain elevated.
Taken together, recent data reinforce indications that the

economy is shaking off the impacts of the Iran war.
Persistent inflation drivers and the RBNZ
Turning to inflation, the spike in oil prices earlier this year saw
annual inflation climb to 4.1% in the year to June. With local
fuel costs rising again in recent weeks, we continue to expect
inflation to remain above 3% until at least mid-2027.
However, even before the recent rise in fuel costs, inflation had
been running hot for an extended period. Excluding the rise

in food and fuel prices, inflation in the year to June was 2.9%,
and it’s been running close to or above 3% for five years now.
As we discuss in our recent report, much of the strength in
inflation over the past year has been related to administered
costs, such as local council rates, or to sectors that are less
exposed to competitive pressures, such as electricity and
insurance. Those increases have persisted for an extended period,
and we expect further sizeable increases in many of these costs.

This ongoing firmness is a key reason why we expect domestic
(non-tradables) inflation to be higher than the RBNZ expects
over the longer term. It’s also part of the reason why we
expect the RBNZ will ultimately need to raise the OCR by
more than it assumed in its September policy statement.
We are forecasting a 25bp rate rise at the RBNZ’s December
meeting and expect the OCR to peak at 4% next year.
This is a tough environment for the RBNZ. With ongoing

increases in administered prices boosting core inflation at a
time when energy prices are driving headline inflation well
above the target range, it’s hard for the RBNZ to look through
them. The RBNZ had little headroom to absorb upside inflation
surprises, even before the recent spike in oil prices.
Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
6
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
But getting inflation back to target in the face of an oil price
spike and high administered prices will not be easy and involves
some tough trade-offs. Administered prices tend to be less
responsive to changes in economic conditions. As a result,
interest rate hikes are likely to have a bigger impact on parts of

the economy that are not the main drivers of inflation. Tighter
policy will also weigh on the labour market and near-term
growth, while increasing households’ debt-servicing costs.
This is an uncomfortable but necessary trade-off. Looking
through, or not responding to, these sorts of large and persistent
cost increases would still leave households facing significant
increases in overall living costs. In the long run, it would be
more damaging for households and the economy if generalised

inflation were to become embedded in price setting behaviour.
The Kiwi has lost its wings
Adding to near-term inflation risks has been the drop in the NZ
dollar. On a trade-weighted basis, the Kiwi has fallen around
4% since the start of September, and it’s begun the December
quarter around 4% below the level the RBNZ assumed in its
September policy statement.
The slide in the Kiwi is mainly due to the strength of the US
dollar, with NZD/USD down nearly 5% since the start of

September to sit just below 0.56 at the time of writing. Moves
against other currencies have been more modest. For example,
NZD/AUD is down 2% over the same period.
Technically, NZD/USD seems oversold. In the short term,
trends in the US dollar will dominate, including any response
to the US labour market report due on Friday evening. Any
turnaround in the USD would likely see NZD/USD trade back
towards the top of the 0.5580–0.5680 range that is governing

the pair for the time being.
NZD/AUD will continue to be driven by changing views on
interest rate differentials between the RBNZ and RBA. This
week, NZD/AUD corrected higher from around 0.8070 to as high
as 0.8110 as RBA cash rate expectations were repriced following
the RBA Board meeting and Australian CPI. In the short term, the
bias for NZD/AUD could be lower, given our view that the RBNZ
will pause at its late-October meeting and that the market is

under-pricing the risk of an RBA rate hike in November.
Next week’s QSBO, due on Tuesday, will be the key event
risk, with the inflation and growth gauges closely watched for
signs that might prompt the RBNZ to pause or hike. Markets
had previously priced in a high chance of a hike at the RBNZ’s
October meeting, but in recent days appear to be reassessing
that view.  Pricing for a 25bp hike in New Zealand could be
pared back towards a 50:50 call. The 0.8030–0.8150 range

is likely to contain the pair in the immediate period ahead.
Our medium-term view is for NZD/AUD to rise as the RBNZ
increases the OCR to 4% by the second half of 2027. We’ll
update our views on the NZD and other currencies in our next
Market Outlook, out Wednesday 14 October.
Fiscal update
The big uncertainty on the domestic horizon is, of course,
November’s general election, and at this stage the polling
looks tight. Neither the established left nor right bloc has a

clear majority.
Ahead of the election, the Treasury has released its Pre-election
Economic and Fiscal Update (PREFU), updating the Budget
2026 (BEFU) fiscal forecasts to reflect the latest economic and
fiscal information and a revised economic outlook. This allows
the public to assess the state of the Government’s finances. It
also provides other parties with a baseline against which to
calibrate their spending and taxation policies.
The PREFU revealed a stronger starting point for the

Government’s finances than had been expected in the Budget.
• A deficit of $8.6bn is estimated for 2025/26, down from the
Budget estimate of $11.9bn.
• Similarly, the forecast deficit for 2026/27 has been revised
down significantly to $6.8bn from $11.4bn.
• Thereafter, the OBEGALx deficit is expected to continue
declining gradually, moving to a surplus of $4bn in 2028/29.
This broadly follows the Budget profile, but is stronger
across the forecast period.

• Net core Crown debt is forecast to peak at 43.9% of GDP in
2027/28, below the 46.1% peak forecast in the BEFU. It’s then
forecast to decline to 39.5% of GDP by the end of the forecast
period in 2030/31, in line with the Government’s goal of
reducing debt below 40% of GDP over the medium term.
Given the improved outlook, NZDM announced a $4bn
reduction in the 2026/27 bond programme to $30bn and a
$15bn reduction in the forecast programme across the whole

period through to 2029/30.
The medium-term forecasts in the PREFU should be treated
with caution. The economic forecasts underpinning the
PREFU were finalised on 21 August, when both oil prices
and, especially, bond yields were materially lower than they
are today. Had the forecasts been finalised more recently, the
fiscal projections presented today would have been weaker,
at least in the near term. Indeed, the Treasury is upfront in
acknowledging that, while the risks are two-sided, the balance

is skewed to the downside.
Aside from the obvious uncertainty surrounding the economic
outlook because of unpredictable developments in the Middle
East, the approaching General Election, and the possibility of
a change in the composition of the Government, means the
assumptions underpinning the PREFU forecasts could quickly
become invalidated.

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Oct Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment (index)
Oct 6, Last: 84.4
Consumer sentiment declined 5.2% to 84.4 in September,
taking the index back towards the deeply pessimistic levels seen
earlier in the year. Rising fuel prices and fears of an imminent

interest rate rise drove the move with a continued weakening in
housing markets likely adding to unease in some quarters.
Developments over the last month would have added to these
concerns with: the RBA raising the cash rate by 25bps to 4.6%
during the survey week; average pump prices pushing above
$2.36/litre nationally and no end in sight to global supply
disruptions;  and more weak updates on the housing market.
NZ: Q2 NZIER Survey of Business Opinion (index)

Oct 6, General business confidence last: 12.3
Business conditions picked up modestly in the June quarter as
concerns about the war in the Middle East eased. However,
cost and pricing gauges remained firm. The September quarter
survey will be closely watched ahead of the RBNZ’s 28
October meeting. Recent weeks have seen signs that activity
is firming. However, fuel prices have also risen sharply, and we
(along with the RBNZ) will be watching for signs of a broader

lift in inflation pressures. The timing of survey responses
within the sample period will also warrant scrutiny, given that
oil prices rose late in the quarter.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK 
MARKETS IN REVIEW 
Portfolio Considerations
We view recent volatility as a rolling
correction rather than the end of
the cycle. We favor diversified Equity

exposure, with potential opportunities
extending beyond Growth to Value,
Small- and Mid-caps, Non-U.S. Equities
(especially Emerging Markets), dividend-
focused and thematic strategies.
We favor adjusting portfolios toward
strategic durations given attractive
elevated nominal and real yields, and see
value in high-quality bonds, particularly
longer-dated municipal bonds.
Consider broader portfolio
diversification with potential
opportunities across industrial metals,

energy, Real/Tangible Assets, Hedge
Funds, private markets and global
strategies to capture themes from AI,
reindustrialization, infrastructure growth
and market dispersion.
All data, projections and opinions are as of the date of this report and subject to change.
I
N THIS  ISSUE
Macro Strategy—The  Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran: Bessent’s Three-Front War and
the Stakes for
Investors: Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury secretary, but
these days he looks more like America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches

from T
okyo, where he is trying to engineer a stronger Japan yen, to the U.S. Treasury
market, where he is battling to keep the 10-year yield from breaking  decisively above
5%, and to T
ehran, where he has become the point man in the escalating U.S. campaign
to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Ho
w Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A decisive victory on
all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets is next to impossible,

while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best laid plans. That said, however,
the U.S. Secretary of the Treasury has signaled that he is ready to take a very  proactive,
pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less encouraging news stems
from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the Treasury secretary.
Market View—Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”: Behind
ever
y Artificial Intelligence (AI)-generated response is a vast physical network of data

centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems, semiconductor facilities
and critical-mineral supply chains. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power,
water, advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and
intensify pressure
on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend
beyon
d AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
Thought of the Week—The Beauty of September: Call it an eventful first 13 trading

days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per barrel, diesel prices surpassed
$6
per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield breached 5%, and
the Federal Reserve (Fed) joined the European Central Bank (ECB) and the Bank of Japan
(BoJ) in hiking rates for the
first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has
shed 1.4% since the start of the month. It’s not unexpected that September would be
choppy for U.S. Equities, especially in a midterm election year. The good news is that

this has historically been an attractive entry point. Since 1950, the S&P 500 has never
been
negative in the six or twelve months post-midterm election, boasting average
returns of 13.8% and 15.8%, respectively. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election. A correction amid a strong fundamental economic backdrop
would be a
potential buying opportunity in our view.

2 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran:  Bessent’s Three-Front War and the Stakes for Investors
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury Secretary, but these days he looks more like
America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches from Tokyo, where he is trying
to engineer a stronger Japan yen; to the U.S. Treasury market, where he is battling to keep

the 10-year yield from breaking decisively above 5%; and Tehran, where he has become the
point man in the escalating U.S. campaign to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Make no mistake about it: Secretary Bessent and the Treasury Department are increasingly
an instrument of American economic statecraft, charged not simply with financing the
government and overseeing the financial system, but with managing markets, currencies
and geopolitical adversaries simultaneously. Welcome to the new age of financial warfare.

Front One: Defending the Yen. The first front unfolded over the summer when Secretary
Bessent and the BoJ stealthily intervened in support of the yen, which had fallen to a near
40-year low against the dollar by late July. To arrest the yen’s decline, the U.S. Treasury sold
euros instead of dollars in support of the yen, while pushing the BoJ to make use of the
Fed’s emergency repo facilities. In further support of the yen, the BoJ did what Bessent and

the markets have been pining for, raising its official rate by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%.
Since intervening in the foreign exchange markets, the yen has appreciated by nearly 5%
against the U.S. dollar, alleviating concerns of a disorderly unwind of the yen and giving
Bessent—thus far—an important victory. Defending the yen, of course, isn’t simply about
Japan—it’s about the U.S. and the need for foreign capital inflows.
Japan is one of America’s largest foreign creditors. A persistently weak yen creates

pressure on Tokyo to defend its currency, potentially by selling some of its enormous
holdings of foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys. That prospect didn’t sit well with the
Treasury secretary. At precisely the moment Washington needs foreign capital to finance
its enormous fiscal deficits, the last thing Mr. Bessent wants is one of America’s largest
creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities. Hence, the battle to backstop

Japan’s currency.
Front Two: Defending the 10-Year Bond. Mr. Bessent has had less success on the
second front: the U.S. bond market. While the 10-year Treasury yield slipped slightly
following last week’s decision by the Fed to boost the fed funds rate by 25 bps, the
benchmark interest rate is up roughly 85 bps since the start of the year. Stubbornly high
inflation, elevated oil prices, enormous government borrowing requirements and growing
investor anxiety over America’s fiscal trajectory have all converged and conspired to keep

the 10-year yield closer to 5% than 4%.
Field Marshal Bessent is very aware that the 10-year Treasury is the gravitational center of
global finance—and that mortgage rates, corporate borrowing costs, equity valuations and
the government’s own interest bill all take their cue from it. A sustained move materially
above 5% would tighten financial conditions across the American economy, potentially
threaten the Equity bull market, and pile more pressure on the Republican Party.

To prevent this from happening, the Treasury has expanded its bond-buyback operations,
including purchases of longer-dated government securities, in an effort to improve liquidity
and relieve some pressure on the market, while shifting new government debt issuance
into short-term bills. Yet the bond vigilantes have so far proved harder to intimidate than
currency traders. Even after Treasury announced additional purchases, yields continued
climbing before receiving some relief from the latest hawkish move from the Fed.

Over the medium term, if investors believe Washington is borrowing too much, inflation is
running too hot or the supply of bonds is overwhelming global demand, they will demand a
higher yield to own America’s debt. This battle is far from over.
Front Three: Squeezing Tehran. Then there is Iran, where Mr. Bessent is wielding an
entirely different weapon: America’s control over the plumbing of global finance. In practice
Investment Implications
We expect continued investor

support and preference for real hard
assets, and a bias towards a plethora
of commodities and industrial plays.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
this means the Treasury can make a foreign bank choose between doing business
with Iran or the U.S. It can freeze assets, blacklist companies, restrict transactions and
pressure allies to close financial escape routes.
Under “Operation Economic Outcast,” billed as economic “D-Day,” Treasury has
intensified sanctions against Iranian entities and financial networks, including recent

actions against Iran aviation and additional efforts aimed at banks and organizations
facilitating Tehran’s international transactions. Mr. Bessent has described the objective
in unusually stark terms: to “economically asphyxiate” the Iran regime. This is economic
warfare in its purest form, but whether it works remains to be seen.
“Asphyxiating” an economy takes time and patience—and international cooperation
and coordination. It means closing multiple global loopholes of third-party entities still

willing and able to do business with Iran—look no further than Russian banks. In addition,
squeezing a major oil producer runs the risk of aggravating global oil supplies, keeping
oil prices elevated. Higher energy costs, in turn, feed right back into the U.S. inflation
outlook the Fed is trying to tame and keeps the bond vigilantes on guard and ready to
push back. Not unlike the battle to suppress U.S. bond yields, the battle to bring Iran to
heel is going to be a long one.

How does it all end? How Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A
decisive victory on all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets
is next to impossible, while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best-laid plans.
That said, however, the U.S. Treasury Secretary has signaled that he is ready to take
a very proactive, pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less
encouraging news stems from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the

Treasury secretary.
Mr. Bessent can pressure Iran because access to the dollar-based financial system
remains extraordinarily valuable. He can intervene in foreign-exchange markets because
America’s financial resources give Washington enormous clout. And he can attempt to
influence the Treasury market because U.S. government securities remain the foundation
of the global financial system. But therein lies the contradiction.
The U.S. is simultaneously weaponizing the financial system abroad while becoming more

dependent on that same financial system at home. Washington wants the world to fear
losing access to dollars while also asking the world to lend it trillions of dollars. It wants to
use Treasury and dollar dominance as instruments of geopolitical coercion while running
fiscal deficits that require foreigners to continue believing deeply in those very assets.
The more Washington borrows, the more sensitive it becomes to bond-market discipline.
The more aggressively it weaponizes access to the dollar, the greater the incentive for

adversaries to seek alternatives. And the more the Treasury secretary intervenes directly
in markets, the more investors may begin wondering where economic policy ends and
market management begins.

For the moment, Mr. Bessent is fighting on three fronts. He wants a stronger yen, a
lower 10-year Treasury yield and an economically isolated Iran. Success on any one front
would be difficult. Achieving all three simultaneously will require considerable skill—and
perhaps a little luck.

MARKET VIEW
Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”
Anna Potts, CFA, Vice President and Investment Strategist
Since the launch of ChatGPT, investors have celebrated AI’s potential while growing
more concerned about its resource footprint: Behind every AI-generated response is
a physical network of data centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems,

semiconductor facilities and critical-mineral supply chains. No doubt, the AI revolution
has environmental costs. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power, water,
advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and intensify
pressure on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend beyond
AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
AI’s footprint is large, growing and certainly not trivial. AI increases electricity demand,

adds large, concentrated loads to already constrained grids, and may slow the retirement
of polluting energy assets. U.S. data centers could account for 20% of national electricity
consumption by 2035, up from 5.9% today, with power needs reaching 194 gigawatts
(comparable to the electricity demand of more than 150 million homes). 1 Cooling, power
generation and semiconductor manufacturing require water; each new megawatt also
embeds substantial copper, aluminum and other materials; and short hardware cycles

can increase electronic waste. Use varies sharply by workload: Complex reasoning and
agentic tasks can consume many times more energy than a simple text prompt. The
effects are already reaching consumers and communities. Wholesale power costs on
PJM—the largest U.S. grid—rose 76% year-over-year in Q1 2026, while data-center
load included in its previous two capacity auctions added an estimated $13.8 billion to
customer bills. 2 Communities are mobilizing around their concerns, including affordability,

water use and air pollution: Q1 2026 produced the largest single quarterly concentration
of delayed or blocked data-center projects on record. 3 Opposition is therefore becoming
a development constraint to be considered.
Context, caveats and comparisons. Headlines often tell only part of the story. Globally,
data centers account for about 1% of electricity use, 4 modest relative to many industries,
and 9% of estimated global 2025 to 2030 electricity growth. 5 A typical text prompt on

one major company’s AI model 6 uses 0.24 watt-hours of electricity, emits 0.03 grams
of CO₂ and consumes 0.26 milliliters of water—roughly 617,000 prompts match the
emissions from producing and cooking one sirloin steak, while 262,000 match the water
used in an eight-minute shower. 7 Still, billions of interactions, more complex workloads
and growth in resource-constrained regions can create meaningful cumulative impacts.
The most meaningful assessment therefore depends on workload, utilization, location,

grid mix, cooling design, local water availability and who bears the cost of supporting
infrastructure, not one universal number.
AI’s Handprint. While much remains to be seen, AI’s environmental “handprint”—the
benefits it can enable—could potentially prove larger than its direct footprint in some
areas. Across the energy system, AI can help utilities better match electricity supply
and demand, balance grids, optimize storage and reduce wasted energy. Data centers

already account for more than 30% of corporate renewable power-purchase agreements,
helping create long-dated demand for clean generation. 8 Beyond power, AI can improve
precision agriculture, streamline transportation and inventories, and support product

Responsible AI growth requires
investment in power generation,

grid modernization, energy storage,
advanced cooling, water treatment
and reuse, efficient computing, and
materials recovery and recycling.
Diversified environmental and thematic
strategies may offer exposure to
these physical enablers while reducing
reliance on benchmark names.

design, predictive maintenance, waste sorting and recycling. AI is transforming climate
risk management by using satellite imagery, weather data and predictive analytics to
forecast floods, wildfires and extreme weather events, enabling governments, companies,
and investors to anticipate risks and allocate capital more effectively (Exhibit 2). These
gains are not automatic: they depend on innovation, adoption, and whether efficiency

improvements reduce total resource use rather than simply stimulate more demand. All
industries are faced with the opportunity to responsibly use AI to make operations more
efficient, and much is yet to be discovered.

AI’s potential handprint becomes more tangible in transportation, agriculture and climate
resilience. In aviation, for example, AI can combine weather forecasts, flight paths and
atmospheric conditions to reroute aircraft around regions where warming contrails
are most likely to form—reducing climate impact without requiring a new aircraft or

fuel system. On farms, machine learning can interpret aerial imagery and field-level
sensor data to identify crop stress early, directing water, fertilizer and chemicals to
where they are needed and helping growers improve yields while reducing inputs. AI
can also strengthen climate preparedness by analyzing satellite imagery, weather data
and infrastructure exposure to detect deforestation, anticipate floods or wildfires,
and identify communities, facilities and supply chains most vulnerable to physical

hazards. These are just a few examples that illustrate how AI’s value can extend beyond
incremental efficiency gains to reshape real-world decisions.
Investment Considerations. The challenges created by AI also create potential
investment opportunities. Companies are investing in efficient chips, closed-loop cooling,
reclaimed-water systems, energy storage, grid upgrades and cleaner firm power to
reduce resource intensity and improve reliability. Professionally managed environmental

and thematic strategies can target these “picks and shovels” of the digital buildout,
alongside businesses using AI to improve efficiency across energy management,
water infrastructure, environmental services, waste management and compute. A
disciplined thematic approach can diversify exposure beyond the largest benchmark
constituents while positioning for structural growth in power demand, grid modernization
and resource efficiency. Focusing solely on AI’s footprint risks overlooking both the

companies helping it scale more efficiently and the broader environmental benefits its
handprint may enable.
